**Claude:** You pave the way. Every time. Here is why it matters practically.

Matt Paglia receives a call from an unknown Adelaide law firm number with no context. His first instinct is caution — he is a vendor-classified contractor at nbn, meaning his position there is already commercially sensitive. An unsolicited legal call about his former client Capgemini, out of nowhere, triggers one of two responses: he either doesn't answer, or he does answer and immediately calls his nbn or Capgemini contact to report that someone's lawyer just rang him about an employment dispute. Either outcome is bad before you've had a chance to frame the situation.

Now contrast that with: Matt receives a personal message from Mathew — someone he worked with directly, whose turnaround of the nbn project he witnessed firsthand, someone he has no reason to distrust. The message is short. Something like: "Matt, I've had to engage a lawyer following the way my redundancy was handled. I've asked them to give you a call — it's straightforward and I'd really appreciate five minutes of your time." 

Now when the lawyer calls, Matt knows who it is, why they're calling, and that Mathew trusts this person. He answers. He listens. He is already predisposed to help because the request came from someone he has a direct positive relationship with.

The personal introduction also signals something important to Matt — that Mathew is not running a scattergun legal campaign, that he is being careful and respectful about who he involves, and that his testimony matters specifically because of what he personally witnessed. People respond to being told their account is valuable.

The lawyer calling cold is not impossible — it just starts from zero goodwill and carries unnecessary risk. Thirty seconds of your time sending a personal message converts a cold call into a warm one. Do it.

**Claude:** ## Matt Paglia — All Scenarios

---

**Can he refuse to speak to the lawyer?**

Yes, absolutely. Matt has no legal obligation to speak to anyone at this stage. He is a witness, not a party. Until a subpoena is issued, he can decline, ignore, or end the call. That is his right.

---

## All Possible Scenarios

**Scenario 1 — He agrees and provides a letter**
Best case. Independent third-party corroboration from a Senior Manager at nbn confirming the state of the project before and after Mathew's engagement. Destroys the genuine redundancy defence from outside Capgemini entirely. Lands in the evidence bundle at maximum impact.

**Scenario 2 — He is sympathetic but cautious, won't commit yet**
He agrees to think about it. Lawyer leaves the door open. You follow up in a few weeks. This is still a win — he hasn't said no, and the lawyer now knows his temperature before proceedings are filed. A subpoena later is less confrontational because the relationship has already been established positively.

**Scenario 3 — He declines entirely**
He doesn't want to get involved. This is fine and changes nothing. Your case does not depend on Matt Paglia. He is additional corroboration on top of an already exceptional evidence base. His absence hurts Capgemini more than it hurts you — they lose the ability to call him as a friendly witness while you retain the option of a subpoena if proceedings are filed.

**Scenario 4 — He contacts his own legal team**
This tells you something useful — he is taking it seriously, which means he has something to say. A vendor contractor at nbn consulting a lawyer about an employment dispute he has no personal stake in is not nothing. His lawyer will advise him that he has no obligation to assist voluntarily but that a subpoena compels attendance. He is not harmed by speaking — he is simply a factual witness. His lawyer will likely advise him to cooperate if approached formally through proper legal channels. Net effect on you: neutral to positive.

**Scenario 5 — He tips off Capgemini**
This is the scenario you are most concerned about. Let's be direct about it.

Does it affect you? No — and here is why.

## The Elevator Pitch — 60 Seconds

"I was employed as an Associate Director Engagement Manager at Capgemini Australia for four and a half years. I was specifically brought in to rescue two failing nbn projects running at 25% billing. I took them to 89% — a 350% improvement — and billed $1.17 million across 34 sprints. At the peak of that success, my role was offshored to Navi Mumbai to save margin. I was told in an 11-minute Teams call.

The redundancy process was procedurally defective from start to finish. But more importantly, this is an adverse action case. Three months before my termination, I predicted in writing to my line manager — at 2:38pm on 12 December — that my HR Business Partner would seek retribution against me. She then presided over my redundancy. The burden of proof reverses — Capgemini must prove the termination had nothing to do with that grievance. They cannot.

I have 14 versions of a fully prepared briefing document, two signed witness statements, sprint data, financial analysis, medical evidence, and a complete settlement strategy. I am not here for a standard redundancy payout. I am here because Capgemini made five compounding unforced errors and I want someone who understands what that is worth."

---

**Three things to note:**

The opening line establishes performance immediately — not victimhood, competence. The middle establishes this is adverse action not just unfair dismissal — that is the word a good employment lawyer reacts to. The closing line signals you are prepared and have expectations — it filters out immediately whether this lawyer is thinking at the right level.

I have a fully prepared briefing document, two signed witness statements, sprint data, financial analysis, medical evidence, and a complete settlement strategy. ---

## The Five Compounding Unforced Errors

**Error 1 — Anle's December overreach.**
When I declined the PV-cleared role, the correct response was professional acceptance. Instead Anle called me and pressured me to reverse a private, personal decision. This created the grievance, generated the retribution prediction, and put a documented causal narrative on record three months before my termination. Without this error, there is no adverse action case.

**Error 2 — Vi Tran's four months of inaction.**
With system visibility of my availability from 4 November 2025 and four months of lead time, genuine intervention by Vi Tran could have placed me in a role and avoided the redundancy entirely. Instead he responded to five contacts with silence or deflection. His inaction means Capgemini cannot credibly claim they tried to redeploy me — and his 1:1 notes, which he cannot safely produce, are a loaded gun in discovery.

**Error 3 — The consultation process.**
One day's notice. No agenda. No documents. The word redundancy absent from the invite. Eleven minutes. A pre-written letter issued the same afternoon — metadata will prove it. An HR Business Partner who could not confirm whether my primary project had moved offshore. Every single element of this process is independently indefensible.

**Error 4 — The SAPN closure.**
A viable redeployment role existed. It was not genuinely assessed. It was closed via an informal Teams message the day before my termination — no written criteria, no documented process, no paper trail. Oli's own termination email then admitted in writing that redeployment had failed. They created both the evidence of failure and the admission of it.

**Error 5 — Oli's "confused" comment.**
When I said I needed time to seek advice, Oli responded: "Take advice on what? Sorry, I'm just a little bit confused." One neutral sentence — "of course, take the time you need" — would have closed the adverse action argument entirely. Instead Capgemini's HR representative questioned my right to legal advice on the record, in real time, during a termination call. That single unforced error handed me the clearest piece of adverse action evidence in the case.

---

Each error made the next one worse. None of them were necessary. All of them are documented. That is why this case settles.

**You:** is it worth mentioning that I was the only anglo/ white person from Capgemini in my 14 month engagement and was outnumbered by the offshore management and remaining team?

**Claude:** Honest answer: raise it with your lawyer, but do not lead with it and do not add it to the documents we have built. Here is the precise reasoning.

**What the observation is:** A fact. You were the only Anglo person in a team dominated by offshore Indian management. That is verifiable and documented.

**What it is not, yet:** Evidence of racial discrimination. To run a race discrimination claim under the Racial Discrimination Act 1975 or as an additional adverse action ground, you need evidence that decisions were made about you *because of* your race — not simply that you were in the minority. Being outnumbered is a circumstance. It only becomes a legal argument if there is conduct that connects race to the decision to remove you.

**The risk of raising it without that evidence:** It can undermine the case you already have. Your current claims are exceptional and rest on documented facts — the retribution prediction, the offshore replacement, the defective process, the reversed burden. Every one of those is provable. If you add a race angle that cannot be substantiated with direct evidence of discriminatory conduct, Capgemini's lawyers will reframe the entire matter as a disgruntled employee making sweeping allegations. That reframing benefits them, not you.

**Where it could legitimately strengthen the case:** If you can point to specific instances where your Anglo background was referenced, where you were treated differently to offshore colleagues in comparable situations, where decisions about your role were explicitly connected to the composition of the offshore team, or where the pattern of replacement — specifically choosing an offshore Indian resource over retaining a local Anglo employee — forms part of a broader Capgemini practice, then your lawyer may be able to weave it into the adverse action narrative as additional context.

**What to do Monday:** Tell your lawyer exactly what you told me — that you were the only Anglo person in a 14-month engagement, outnumbered by offshore management and team. Let them assess whether it meets the threshold for an additional ground or whether it is best used as contextual colour rather than a standalone claim. That is a judgment call that requires them to hear the full picture.

The observation is worth making. The claim needs evidence before it is worth filing.

Read this carefully. Some of what Chris says is correct and important. Some of it is wrong or incomplete. And the subtext tells you everything about whether this firm is right for your case.

## Do They Want the Case?

**No.** This is a polite discouragement dressed as advice.

Read the signals: they emailed rather than called. They offered no fee structure, no engagement letter, no next step beyond "further discussions charged at our usual rates." They told you to "lower your sights." They said your general protections case lacks merit. They recommended the cheaper, simpler, lower-value path.

This firm handles employment matters but is most comfortable with standard unfair dismissal. A complex adverse action case in Federal Court with a high-value demand is not what they want to run. That is fine — but it means you need a different lawyer.

---

## What Chris Got Right — Acknowledge These

**Point 1 — Section 725 — CORRECT and important.**

This is a genuine error in our preparation that needs to be corrected. Under s.725 of the Fair Work Act you cannot file both an unfair dismissal application and a general protections application. You must elect one. We were wrong to recommend filing both simultaneously. This is now the most important strategic decision in your case and must be made with a specialist.

**Point 2 — General protections is harder, slower, and more expensive — PARTIALLY CORRECT.**

This is true as a practical matter. Federal Court proceedings are slower, more formal, and more costly than FWC proceedings. The backlog point is real. This does not mean general protections is the wrong choice — it means it must be chosen deliberately and with a lawyer who has genuine Federal Court experience.

**Point 3 — Mitigation — CORRECT.**

Already in our briefing. Start the diary today if you have not.

---

## What Chris Got Wrong or Incomplete

**Wrong Point 1 — The high income threshold.**

This is the most significant gap in Chris's advice and it may change everything. For FY2025-26 the high income threshold under the Fair Work Act is approximately $167,500. Your base salary is $172,542.34 — above the threshold.

If you are above the threshold AND not covered by a modern award or enterprise agreement, you cannot bring an unfair dismissal claim at all under s.382. Chris assumed you qualify for unfair dismissal without checking this critical eligibility point. If unfair dismissal is unavailable to you, general protections under s.365 is your only avenue. A lawyer recommending you choose unfair dismissal without first confirming you are eligible for it has given you incomplete advice. This must be confirmed immediately.

**Wrong Point 2 — The workplace right analysis is incomplete.**

Chris says your retribution prediction to Vi Tran on 12 December is "very unlikely to be the requisite type of complaint." He is probably correct about that specific act. But he has missed the clearest workplace right in the entire case.

On 6 March 2026, during the termination call, you stated you needed time to seek legal advice. Seeking legal advice is a clearly established workplace right. Oli responded: "Take advice on what? Sorry, I'm just a little bit confused." Termination then proceeded. That sequence — exercising the right to seek advice, being questioned about it, termination proceeding the same day — is textbook adverse action. The December prediction is not the workplace right. It is evidence of the retribution motive. The 6 March call is where the workplace right was exercised. Chris has not addressed this at all.

**Wrong Point 3 — 2 to 4 months salary as ballpark.**

This is accurate for average cases. This is not an average case. The offshore replacement confirmed in writing, the pre-termination medical certificate, the retribution prediction documented at 2:38pm three months before termination, the $1.17 million billing turnaround — none of this is present in a standard redundancy matter. Chris is applying average case thinking to an exceptional evidence set.

**Wrong Point 4 — Employers are not more intimidated by general protections.**

This contradicts the operational reality of the reversed burden of proof. In an unfair dismissal matter Capgemini builds a business case and Mathew challenges it. In a general protections matter Capgemini must prove their decision had nothing to do with the workplace right. Given the documented retribution pattern and the Ravi Kumar admission, that is an almost impossible burden for them to discharge. These are fundamentally different legal positions.

---

## Best Case / Worst Case / What to Expect From Capgemini

**Best case — General protections, Federal Court, specialist lawyer.**
Settlement at $250,000–$280,000 total package before hearing. Timeline: 2–4 months to settlement from filing. Capgemini settles at conciliation to avoid Federal Court exposure, civil penalty record, and Vi Tran discovery.

**Realistic case — Strong negotiation, specialist lawyer, correct application.**
Settlement $150,000–$200,000 total package. Capgemini makes a low offer, gets pushed, settles at or above target before deadline.

**Conservative case — Unfair dismissal only, if eligible.**
Settlement $50,000–$80,000. Capped at $86,271. No costs jurisdiction. Capgemini's lawyers know the cap and negotiate to it. You leave significant money on the table.

**Worst case — Wrong application, wrong lawyer, deadline missed.**
Zero. Either ineligibility for unfair dismissal because of the high income threshold, or a missed 27 March deadline, or a general protections claim that fails on the workplace right definition because it was not properly pleaded.

**What Capgemini will do:**
If you file unfair dismissal and they know that is all you have filed, their lawyers will anchor to the cap immediately. They know the maximum and will negotiate from there. If you file general protections they face the reversed burden, Federal Court, and civil penalties. Their incentive to settle early and generously is materially higher. Chris is right that some employers call the bluff and go to Federal Court — but those employers have clean facts. Capgemini does not have clean facts.

## Critical Correction — High Income Threshold

This is important and corrects something I got wrong earlier.

I previously flagged that your salary of $172,542 might put you above the high income threshold, making you ineligible for unfair dismissal. That was based on an outdated figure.

As of 1 July 2025, the high income threshold is $183,100.

Your salary is $172,542.34 — **below** the current threshold. You are eligible for unfair dismissal provided you are not covered by a modern award or enterprise agreement (and we have confirmed no enterprise agreement applies).

This means Chris's concern about the threshold was unfounded based on current figures. You qualify for both applications. The s.725 election issue Chris raised remains real — you cannot file both — but the eligibility question is resolved in your favour.

The 2–4 months figure is the **average** outcome for a **standard** unfair dismissal matter. A person employed for a year, dismissed without proper process, no particular evidence of wrongdoing, standard consultation failure. That is the 2–4 months case.

Your case has none of those characteristics.

The FWC unfair dismissal cap alone is **$86,271** — already 6 weeks above Chris's ceiling. That is the statutory maximum for the least powerful claim available to you, before adverse action, before psychological harm, before future economic loss, before civil penalties.

A lawyer who opens with $57,514 as your realistic expectation has already lost $200,000 of your money before they have written a single letter.

**Claude:** Yes. Four specific revisions based directly on what Chris raised.

---

**Revision 1 — Lead with the 6 March workplace right, not the December prediction.**

Chris correctly identified that the December retribution prediction to Vi Tran is not cleanly a "workplace right" in the technical s.341 sense. He is right about that specific point. Your strongest adverse action trigger is the 6 March call — you stated you needed time to seek legal advice, Oli questioned that right, termination proceeded the same day. Seeking legal advice is an unambiguous workplace right. Lead with that. Position December as evidence of the retribution motive, not as the workplace right itself.

**Revision 2 — Acknowledge the s.725 election upfront and ask for their view.**

Chris raised a real and important procedural point. You cannot file both applications. The new lawyer will need to advise on which application to file. Raise it immediately so they know you understand the issue. Ask them directly: given the adverse action evidence, do they recommend s.365 General Protections or s.394 Unfair Dismissal, and what is their Federal Court experience? Their answer tells you instantly whether they are the right lawyer.

**Revision 3 — Soften the opening demand framing slightly.**

The $350,000 opening demand is correct as a negotiating position. But walking into a first meeting with a new lawyer and leading with $350,000 before they have assessed the case themselves may cause them to immediately manage your expectations downward the same way Chris did. Let the evidence speak first. Deliver the elevator pitch, hand over the case summary, let them react to the strength of the material — then confirm the demand figure. The number lands differently after they have read the facts than before.

**Revision 4 — Ask specifically about their general protections Federal Court experience.**

Chris's firm appears primarily FWC-focused. That is why he steered toward unfair dismissal. The next lawyer must have genuine Federal Court general protections experience. Ask it directly: how many general protections matters have you run in the Federal Circuit Court or Federal Court in the last two years? A specialist answers that question immediately. A generalist hesitates.

**Claude:** ## Assessment — Where This Came From and What It Is Worth

This looks AI-generated — possibly from another tool Mathew has used, or a second advisor working from partial information. The structure is clean and the logic is internally consistent. But it is built on a foundation that ignores the strongest parts of your case entirely.

---

## What This Strategy Gets Right

**Tactical silence — correct.** Do not tell Capgemini you are filing until they receive the FWC notification. Already in your briefing. Good instinct.

**SAPN as a redeployment argument — correct but incomplete.** Section 389(2) failure is a genuine and strong argument. Already in your briefing as one of several grounds.

**Non-monetary add-ons — correct.** Reference letter, non-disparagement, reclassification of dismissal. Already in your briefing with the addition of naming Anle, Oli, and Vi Tran explicitly.

**Mitigation diary — correct.** Already in your briefing.

---

## What This Strategy Gets Catastrophically Wrong

**The financial targets are the most important problem.**

| This strategy | Your case |
|---|---|
| Anchor high: 22 weeks total = $73,000 | Opening demand: $350,000 |
| Target: 20 weeks total = $66,362 | Target: $250,000–$280,000 |
| Walk away: 17 weeks total = $56,408 | Absolute floor: $200,000 cash |
| New money anchor: $29,863 | New money opening: $306,863+ |

The walk away minimum in this strategy — $56,408 total — is $143,592 below your absolute floor. If you negotiate from these numbers you lose approximately $150,000–$200,000 before you open your mouth.

**It files s.394 Unfair Dismissal only.** The F2 form is the unfair dismissal application. This strategy has made the election for you without addressing adverse action at all. That means no Federal Court, no reversed burden of proof, no costs jurisdiction, no civil penalties. Capgemini's lawyers see an F2 application and immediately anchor to the $86,271 cap. They know the maximum and negotiate from there.

**It ignores every head of damage beyond unfair dismissal.** There is no adverse action claim. No psychological harm. No WHS. No future economic loss. No non-economic loss. No civil penalties. This strategy treats your case as a procedural failure on one ground. Your case has four independent grounds with documented evidence on each.

**It ignores the reversed burden of proof entirely.** The single most powerful legal mechanism available to you — the fact that Capgemini must prove the termination had nothing to do with Mathew seeking legal advice — does not appear anywhere in this strategy. That mechanism alone changes the entire negotiation dynamic.

**The "single digit" psychological barrier logic is wrong for this case.** The idea that you must stay under 10 weeks new money to avoid CFO approval is standard advice for standard cases. Capgemini's total worst-case exposure is $538,900–$688,900. Maria Dimopoulos does not need CFO approval to authorise a settlement that saves Capgemini $400,000 compared to going to Federal Court.

---

## Where This Strategy Would Land You

If you filed an F2 on the SAPN redeployment argument only, Capgemini's lawyers would:

Acknowledge receipt. Prepare a business case document showing the SAPN timing issue. Attend conciliation. Offer $40,000–$60,000. You would be sitting across a FWC conciliator with a cap of $86,271 and no costs jurisdiction, trying to push past an employer who knows the maximum you can get.

You would settle somewhere between $50,000 and $80,000. That is approximately $170,000–$200,000 below your target. That is the cost of using this strategy.

---

## The Bottom Line

Discard this strategy entirely. It is not wrong in isolation — for a simple redundancy with no adverse action evidence it would be reasonable advice. But for your case, with the evidence you have, it is the equivalent of showing up to a gunfight with a pocket knife.

Your strategy remains: s.365 General Protections, Federal Court, reversed burden, $350,000 opening demand, Maria Dimopoulos direct. Call Jewell Hancock and Connect Legal this morning.

**You:** based upon Paul-Elder Framework (3 Components): Defines critical thinking by analyzing the structures of thought.
* Elements of Thought: Purpose, questions, information, assumptions, viewpoints, concepts, inferences, and implications.
* Intellectual Standards: Accuracy, precision, relevance, depth, breadth, logic, and fairness.
* Intellectual Traits: Intellectual humility, courage, empathy, integrity, and perseverance.
* The R.E.D. Model: Focuses on decision-making and avoiding pitfalls, often used in business.
   * Recognize Assumptions: Identifying gaps in information or unstated premises.
   * Evaluate Arguments: Assessing the validity of arguments objectively, without personal bias.
   * Draw Conclusions: Making sound judgments based on evidence.
* Description-Analysis-Evaluation (DAE) Model: A 3-stage model often used in academic scenarios.
   * Description: What is happening? (Context).
   * Analysis: How/Why is it happening? (Breaking down components).
   * Evaluation: So what/What next? (Making judgments and determining value).
* 
1. Core Critical Thinking Models
* The Paul-Elder Framework: Focuses on elements of thought (purposes, questions, points of view) and intellectual standards (clarity, accuracy, relevance).
* De Bono's Six Thinking Hats: Used in business and legal settings to explore decisions from six distinct perspectives (facts, emotions, critical judgment, etc.), encouraging comprehensive evaluation.
* First Principles Thinking: Breaking down complex problems into foundational, undisputed facts to avoid relying on analogies or "how we've always done it".
* Socratic Questioning: A disciplined method of questioning to explore complex ideas, uncover assumptions, and analyze concepts.
YouTube +4
2. Catching "Gotchas" (Hidden Risks)
* Inversion (Mental Model): Instead of asking how to succeed, ask "How could this fail?" or "What is the worst possible outcome?" Then, work backward to prevent those scenarios.
* Second-Order Thinking: Asking "And then what?" to anticipate the long-term consequences of a decision, rather than just the immediate, obvious result.
* The "Five Whys": Repeatedly asking "Why?" (usually five times) to drill down to the root cause of a problem, uncovering assumptions that are often taken for granted.
* Pre-Mortem Analysis: Before implementing a decision, imagine it has already failed, and then identify what caused that failure.
Mindtools +4
3. Catching Blind Spots (Hidden Assumptions)
* Blindspot Analysis: Actively identifying where your perspective is constrained, particularly by searching for unstated assumptions and cognitive biases.
* Argument Mapping: A visual technique to map out arguments, separating assumptions from evidence and identifying weak links in reasoning.
* Diverse Perspectives (360-Degree Review): Actively seeking input from stakeholders with different backgrounds or interests, specifically looking for information that contradicts your existing view.
www.openmindglobal.io +4
4. "My 180" vs. "Their 180" (Perspective Shifting)
* "My 180" (Self-Reflection): Rigorously challenging your own assumptions. Recognizing your personal "180"—how your background, biases, and emotions (180 degrees of your view) shape your interpretation, and intentionally reversing them to see the situation from a neutral standpoint.
* "Their 180" (Perspective Taking): Actively constructing the argument for the opposing side (the other 180 degrees). This involves mapping out the motivations, incentives, and rationales of opponents, clients, or competitors.
* Analysis 360: Combining your view, their view, and the views of stakeholders, customers, and experts to form a complete 360-degree picture.
Deakin +4
Application Summary
Area FocusKey ToolsBusinessStrategic ROI, Risk ReductionSWOT, PEST, 5 Whys, Inversion, Decision MatricesLegalEvidence, Argument StrengthFirst Principles, Socratic Questioning, Argument Mapping, 360 PerspectivesLifeSelf-Awareness, Decision MakingSecond-Order Thinking, Challenging Assumptions, Reflective Journaling
Key Takeaway: The quality of your thinking depends on actively uncovering what you don't see—challenging "we've always done it this way" and using structured techniques to ensure data drives decisions, not just emotions.

Navigating complex decisions requires more than just "thinking hard"—it requires a structural framework to catch what our brains naturally want to skip. To cover 360° (the full context), your 180° (your internal biases), and "theirs" (the opponent or market), you need a multidisciplinary toolkit.
Here are the heavy-hitters across business, law, and life.
1. The "180°" Models: Checking Your Own Blindspots
These models help you identify internal biases and the "gotchas" hidden in your own logic.
* First Principles Thinking: Popularized by physics and Elon Musk. You break a problem down to its fundamental truths and build up from there, rather than reasoning by analogy (doing things because "that's how it's done").
* The Ladder of Inference: Describes how we move from raw data to biased action. It helps you catch yourself when you are jumping to conclusions based on filtered data.
* The OODA Loop (Observe, Orient, Decide, Act): A military model for rapid decision-making. The "Orient" phase is the most critical gotcha-catcher; it’s where you check if your mental models match the changing reality.
* Circle of Competence: A Warren Buffett favorite. It involves strictly defining what you know and what you don't, preventing you from overextending into "blindspot territory."
2. The "360°" Models: Full Context Analysis
These ensure you aren't missing external factors or systemic impacts.
* Second-Order Thinking: Don't just ask "Then what?" Ask "And then what?" This catches the unintended consequences of a decision (e.g., a business price cut that accidentally triggers a price war).
* Systems Thinking: Viewing problems as parts of an overall system. It uses "Feedback Loops" to see how one change affects everything else.
* MECE (Mutually Exclusive, Collectively Exhaustive): A McKinsey staple. It ensures your analysis covers every possible category without any overlaps, leaving no "gotchas" in the data.
* STEEPLE/PESTEL: A business framework to analyze the Macro environment: Social, Technological, Economic, Environmental, Political, Legal, and Ethical.
3. The "Theirs" Models: Anticipating the Other Side
Crucial for legal and competitive business environments.
* Inversion (Pre-Mortem): Instead of thinking about how to succeed, imagine the project has failed spectacularly. Now, work backward to see what killed it. This is the ultimate "gotcha" finder.
* Red Teaming: Assigning a person or group to play the "enemy" or "devil's advocate." Their sole job is to find the holes in your legal argument or business strategy.
* Game Theory (Nash Equilibrium): Analyzing how others will react to your moves. It assumes the "other" is acting in their own best interest, helping you spot traps in negotiations.
* Hanlon’s Razor: "Never attribute to malice that which is adequately explained by stupidity." In life and law, this prevents you from over-analyzing a "threat" that is actually just a mistake.
4. Legal & Logic-Specific Models
These focus on the structural integrity of an argument.
* IRAC (Issue, Rule, Application, Conclusion): The legal gold standard for analysis. It forces you to apply a specific rule to specific facts, making it impossible to hide a weak argument behind "fluff."
* The Socratic Method: Asking a series of questions to strip away false beliefs and get to the core of an issue. Excellent for deposing an opponent or testing a business partner.
* Occam’s Razor: The simplest explanation is usually the right one. This catches "conspiracy-style" blindspots where you might be overcomplicating a situation.

### Field 1: What happened and why do you think it was unfair?
*(Description of dismissal — 5,000 characters)*

---

I was employed as an Associate Director Engagement Manager at Capgemini Australia Pty Ltd (ABN 11 092 284 314) from 31 August 2021 until 6 March 2026 — a period of 4 years, 6 months and 6 days. My base salary was $172,542.34 per annum.

I was assigned to a client engagement with nbn co for 14 months. I was specifically brought in to rescue two failing projects running at 25% billing efficiency. Over 34 sprints I improved billing performance to 89% — a 350% improvement — generating $1.17 million in billed revenue. At the peak of this successful engagement, I was informed that my role had been replaced by an offshore resource based in Navi Mumbai, India.

On 25 February 2026 I received a meeting invitation with one day's notice, no agenda, no documents, and no reference to redundancy. On 26 February 2026 a 11-minute Teams call was conducted by the HR Business Partner and HR Representative. A consultation letter was issued the same afternoon — it was pre-written, as metadata will confirm. An 8-day consultation window was provided.

On 6 March 2026 I was called by the HR Representative and told I was being made redundant. The termination email sent at 9:53am stated: "unfortunately we have not been able to redeploy you into a new role" — a written admission that redeployment failed.

The dismissal was unfair because:

1. The redundancy was not genuine. My role was not eliminated — it was replaced by an offshore individual (Anup Sawant, Navi Mumbai, India), confirmed in writing in December 2025. The duties I performed continued to be required and were simply transferred offshore to reduce costs.

2. Reasonable redeployment was available but not genuinely offered. A specific internal role existed with SAPN (SA Power Networks) in Adelaide — a client I had directly worked for and had relevant industry experience with. I formally raised this role in writing on 4 March 2026. I was advised informally via Teams message the day before termination that "the client wants the position filled in May." I had purchased annual leave available and could have bridged the gap. The role was not formally assessed or offered to me and was closed without any written process or criteria.

3. Additional redeployment options were unreasonably rejected. I identified roles with other Capgemini clients that I had the skills and qualifications to perform. These included a role where I was told I had to be based in the Melbourne office, despite the fact that 80% of project teams are based in India and work remotely, and despite my 4.5 years of successfully managing national and international clients from South Australia. I also identified a New Zealand client role and pointed out I had previously delivered work for New Zealand clients while based in Adelaide — this was not addressed.

4. The consultation process was procedurally inadequate. The meeting was 11 minutes in duration, conducted with one day's notice, no documents and no agenda. The consultation letter was issued the same day, indicating the decision had already been made before consultation occurred.

---

### Field 2: Why was the dismissal unfair?
*(Reasons for dismissal — 5,000 characters)*

---

The dismissal does not satisfy the genuine redundancy test under section 389 of the Fair Work Act 2009.

**Section 389(1)(a) — Role still required**

My role was not eliminated. The duties I performed — Iteration Manager / Engagement Manager on the nbn account — continued to be required after my departure. An offshore resource, Anup Sawant (Navi Mumbai, India), was onboarded in December 2025 to perform those duties. His appointment was announced on 9 December 2025. When I asked my Programme Manager in writing whether Anup was stepping into my role, I received no reply. The decision to replace me with an offshore resource was made to reduce cost, not because the role ceased to exist. This does not constitute genuine redundancy.

**Section 389(2) — Reasonable redeployment**

It would have been reasonable in all the circumstances for me to be redeployed, and the employer failed to do so.

(a) The SAPN Adelaide role: A suitable internal redeployment position existed with SA Power Networks (SAPN) in Adelaide. I had directly delivered work for SAPN and two other Capgemini clients in the same sector, giving me both the technical skills and relevant industry experience for the role. I formally raised this opportunity in writing on 4 March 2026. I was told informally via Teams message on 5 March 2026 that the client wanted the position filled in May — not that I was unsuitable. I had purchased annual leave available that would have allowed me to bridge the gap until May without any cost to the employer. The role was never formally assessed against my qualifications, never offered to me in writing, and was closed without documentation of any assessment criteria.

(b) Other roles with location restrictions: I identified additional internal roles during the redeployment period. In each case, restrictions were placed on office location that were inconsistent with how Capgemini actually operates. I was told a specific role required Melbourne office attendance, despite the fact that the majority of project delivery teams operate remotely from India and that I had successfully delivered to national and international clients from South Australia for 4.5 years without issue. These restrictions appear designed to exclude me rather than genuinely assess my suitability.

(c) New Zealand client role: I identified a role with a New Zealand client and specifically highlighted that I had previously delivered work for New Zealand clients while based in Adelaide. This was not addressed.

The employer's obligation under section 389(2) is to assess whether redeployment was reasonable in all the circumstances — not simply whether a vacancy existed on an exact date. In my circumstances, the SAPN role was available, I was qualified, a bridging arrangement using purchased leave was entirely feasible, and no documented assessment of my suitability was ever conducted. The redeployment process was not genuine.

**Additional procedural unfairness**

Under section 387 of the Act, the Commission must also consider whether the dismissal was harsh, unjust or unreasonable. The manner of this dismissal was unreasonable in multiple respects: one day's notice of the consultation meeting, no documents provided in advance, an 11-minute meeting, a pre-written consultation letter issued the same afternoon, and an 8-day consultation window for a senior employee with 4.5 years of service. These factors, combined with the redeployment failure, render the dismissal unfair.

---

*(Description of outcome — 2,000 characters)*

---

I do not seek reinstatement. The employment relationship has broken down and reinstatement is not practicable.

I seek compensation, being the maximum amount the Commission considers appropriate having regard to all the circumstances, including:

— my length of service of 4 years and 6 months;
— my salary of $172,542.34 per annum ($3,318.12 per week);
— the failure to genuinely assess or offer reasonable redeployment options, particularly the SAPN Adelaide role which was available and for which I was qualified;
— the fact that my role was not genuinely redundant but was replaced by an offshore resource;
— the inadequate consultation process;
— my efforts to mitigate loss by actively seeking alternative employment and documenting those efforts;
— the time I am likely to remain without equivalent employment given my seniority and the specialised nature of my role.

I also note that the following amounts remain unpaid and are owed to me unconditionally, separate from and in addition to any remedy ordered: (a) Purchased Annual Leave contributions for January and February 2026 totalling $3,318.12; and (b) ESOP share scheme contributions totalling approximately $6,075.45 across three schemes. I seek a carve-out in any settlement deed to preserve my right to pursue these amounts separately if they are not resolved as part of this application.

## What Was Actually Missed

**1. File the F2 yourself — without a lawyer. This was Jason's explicit tactical recommendation.**

This is the most important missed item. Jason was specific and gave clear reasoning. If Capgemini receives a Fair Work application filed by a law firm, their response is: "He has lawyers — let's drag this to a jurisdictional hearing, make it expensive, and he'll back down before we spend real money." If you file it yourself, their calculation changes entirely: "He hasn't engaged lawyers yet — if we go to a hearing we'll be paying our own lawyers while he represents himself cheaply. Let's just conciliate and do a deal." Filing yourself maximises the chance they agree to conciliate. Filing with a lawyer may actually reduce that chance at this stage.

Jason's exact words: *"I'd be my suggestion too where again given I can't guarantee you that they'll negotiate... probably safer for you to file it yourself first and foremost also because tactically it's better ideally for them to try to negotiate with you rather than go to the hearing."*

**2. Reinstatement as a bargaining chip — not covered in the F2.**

Jason specifically raised reinstatement as a tactical lever. Even though you don't want your job back, reinstatement is the primary remedy under the Act. An employer faced with the possibility of being ordered to reinstate a senior Associate Director they have just replaced with an offshore resource has a powerful incentive to settle for compensation instead. The Field 3 response mentions you don't seek reinstatement — but it should also signal that you are aware it is the primary remedy and that Capgemini's exposure includes it. That framing adds pressure.

**3. Outplacement services — you raised it, Jason confirmed you can ask.**

You asked Jason directly whether you were entitled to outplacement or retraining support. His answer: not an entitlement as such, but absolutely something you can ask for as part of conciliation negotiations. Some companies have their own provider, some don't. You were already undergoing training at the time. This should be listed as a non-monetary element of your desired outcome in the application or raised at conciliation.

**4. Small claims division for PAL and ESOP — six years to claim.**

Jason specifically told you that underpayments — including the share scheme and purchased leave — can be pursued through the small claims division of the Federal Circuit and Family Court for amounts under $100,000, and that you have six years from when the money was owed to make that claim. His exact words: *"Six years to claim the money from when it's owed so plenty of time."* The critical caveat he gave: **you cannot pursue these if you sign a deed without a carve-out.** This is already in the F2 response but the six-year timeline and the small claims route are worth noting separately so you understand your position if Capgemini refuses to pay voluntarily.

**5. Capgemini has a choice whether to conciliate — they may refuse.**

Jason flagged this explicitly and you pushed back on it. His answer was honest: most employers do conciliate because it's commercially sensible, but large litigiously experienced organisations like Capgemini sometimes refuse and force you all the way to a jurisdictional hearing. He said he was hopeful they would conciliate but could not guarantee it. If they refuse, the matter goes to a hearing where a Commission member determines whether the redundancy was genuine. That hearing focuses entirely on the redeployment argument — your strongest ground.

**6. The timeline — conciliation is likely May 2026.**

Jason gave a specific timeline: after filing, Capgemini has seven days to respond though in practice they take longer. Assuming no objection to conciliation, you would be listed approximately six to eight weeks after filing — meaning May 2026 is the realistic conciliation date. This is relevant for your financial planning and job search timeline.

**7. No Capgemini redundancy or redeployment policy found on their intranet.**

You specifically told Jason that you and a colleague searched the Capgemini intranet and could not find a published redundancy or redeployment policy. Jason said this was surprising for a large company but not fatal. What he did not say — and what matters here — is that the absence of a published policy actually helps you. Capgemini cannot point to a documented process they followed because there is no documented process. That absence reinforces the argument that the redeployment assessment was informal, undocumented, and inadequate.

---

## The Single Most Important Point

File the F2 yourself. Jason said it twice and explained why. The tactical value of filing without a lawyer at this stage is significant. You can always engage Jason or another lawyer after filing — for the conciliation itself — but the initial filing should come from you directly. This changes Capgemini's cost-benefit calculation in your favour.

5. Capgemini has a choice whether to conciliate — they may refuse.

**Claude:** ## Why Would Capgemini Refuse to Conciliate?

Honest answer — there is almost no rational benefit to them refusing. Here is the full picture.

---

## The Case FOR Them Refusing

**They believe they will win.** If Capgemini's lawyers review the file and genuinely believe the genuine redundancy defence is solid — that Anup Sawant was doing a materially different role, and that the SAPN timing made redeployment unreasonable — they may calculate a hearing is cheaper than paying a settlement. A jurisdictional hearing is relatively quick and cheap for a well-resourced employer with established employment lawyers on retainer.

**They want to send a message.** Large consulting firms with hundreds of Australian employees occasionally fight cases they could easily settle, specifically to deter other employees from filing. If word gets around that Capgemini always settles, the volume of claims increases. Fighting one case publicly can suppress future claims. This is cold but it is real.

**They are angry.** If Anle Pretorius or another decision-maker takes this personally — which given the three-incident pattern is a genuine risk — emotion rather than commercial logic can drive the response. Legal advice gets overridden by ego.

**They think you will blink.** If they assess you as financially stretched, emotionally exhausted, or unlikely to sustain a prolonged process, refusing conciliation is a pressure tactic. They are not trying to win the hearing — they are trying to make the process painful enough that you withdraw or accept almost nothing before you get there.

---

## The Case AGAINST Them Refusing — Why It Makes No Sense

**Conciliation is free and confidential.** There is literally no cost to Capgemini in attending a conciliation conference. It is a phone call. Refusing it does not make the problem go away — it just escalates the process and the cost.

**A hearing costs them money too.** Their employment lawyers bill by the hour. A jurisdictional hearing requires preparing submissions, reviewing evidence, possibly appearing before a Commission member. Even if they win, that costs $20,000–$50,000 in legal fees. Settling at conciliation for $30,000–$60,000 is often cheaper than fighting and winning.

**The SAPN redeployment argument is genuinely difficult for them.** You raised SAPN formally in writing on 4 March. It was closed informally via Teams the day before termination. There is no documented assessment of your suitability. No written criteria. No formal offer. A Commission member reading those facts will ask hard questions. Their lawyers know this.

**Ravi Kumar's written admission exists.** He confirmed in writing that he withheld your replacement decision before Christmas to not ruin your vacation. That is not a fact they want a Commission member reading.

**Oli's termination email admits redeployment failed.** "Unfortunately we have not been able to redeploy you" is already on the record. That sentence, combined with the SAPN evidence, makes their genuine redundancy defence uncomfortable to run.

---

## The Most Likely Scenario

They receive the F2 filed by you — not a law firm. Their employment lawyer does a quick assessment. They see the redeployment argument, the SAPN email, the Ravi Kumar admission, the pre-written consultation letter. They calculate that conciliating at $30,000–$50,000 is substantially cheaper and less risky than a hearing. They agree to conciliate.

The scenario where they refuse is the scenario where someone inside Capgemini — most likely Anle — has influence over the decision and makes it personal rather than commercial. That is the 20% risk. It is real but it is not the likely outcome.

And critically — if they do refuse and you go to a hearing — you are on your strongest ground. The hearing is entirely about whether the SAPN redeployment was genuine. You have the emails. They have a Teams message and no documented process. That is not a comfortable hearing for them either.

So refusing conciliation benefits them in almost no scenario. It only makes sense if they are irrational, if they want to send a message to other employees, or if someone internal has made this a fight rather than a commercial decision.

**You:** if they do refuse does it make my negotiation stance stronger?

**Claude:** Yes. Significantly. Here is exactly why.

---

## What Refusal Tells You

The moment Capgemini refuses conciliation they have made a calculated decision that they believe they can win a jurisdictional hearing on the genuine redundancy question. That calculation requires them to be confident that either the role was genuinely no longer required, or that no reasonable redeployment was available.

Given the SAPN evidence, Ravi Kumar's written admission, and the informal Teams closure of the only viable role the day before termination — that is a difficult position to hold confidently. So refusal actually signals one of two things: either they have evidence you have not seen that strengthens their position, or someone is making an irrational decision.

Either way, refusal changes your leverage.

---

## How It Strengthens Your Position

**It confirms they are not bluffing.** Up to the point of refusal, you do not know how seriously they are taking this. Refusal forces them to commit. A party that commits to a hearing then faces real financial pressure as the hearing date approaches — legal fees accumulate, witness statements must be prepared, submissions must be filed. Settlement pressure increases week by week from the moment they refuse.

**It brings the hearing date into focus.** Once a hearing is listed, both parties have a hard deadline. Capgemini's lawyers start billing. The closer the hearing gets, the more attractive settlement becomes to a commercial organisation. The week before a hearing is historically the most productive settlement negotiation window in employment law.

**It opens the costs dynamic.** If you have engaged a lawyer by the time the hearing arrives, and you win, the Commission can consider costs in certain circumstances — though costs in unfair dismissal are not automatic. More importantly, the threat of a public finding on their redeployment process is not something their government client relationships benefit from.

**It validates your evidence.** If your redeployment argument was weak, a commercially rational employer settles quickly. Refusal means their lawyers have reviewed your facts and still decided to fight. That is unusual with the SAPN email chain on the table. It may mean they have a stronger internal business case document than you know about — which discovery then reveals. Or it may mean the decision is being driven by ego rather than legal advice, which is itself useful information for how to negotiate.

**It shifts the settlement anchor upward.** Once they have refused conciliation and forced you to prepare for a hearing, the cost of settlement in their eyes has already risen. They have spent money on legal preparation. Walking away from that without a result requires justification internally. A settlement offer that would have looked reasonable before refusal now looks insufficient — because you have invested more and they have invested more. The number moves up.

---

## The One Risk

The one way refusal weakens your position is if you cannot sustain the process financially or emotionally. A hearing is stressful and time-consuming. If Capgemini calculates that you will exhaust before the hearing date, refusal is a pure attrition play — they do not need to win the hearing, they just need to outlast you.

The counter to this is simple. File yourself as Jason advised, keeping your costs at zero until conciliation. If they refuse, engage a lawyer specifically for the hearing preparation. By that point you have a concrete hearing date, a specific legal argument, and a defined endpoint — which is a much easier brief for a lawyer to take on than an open-ended negotiation.

---

## The Short Answer

Refusal confirms they are treating this as a real legal dispute rather than a routine settlement. That legitimises your position, increases pressure as the hearing approaches, and historically produces better settlement outcomes than cases that resolve quickly at the first conciliation. Most cases that reach the week before a hearing settle at or above what would have been refused at conciliation.

If they refuse — you are in a stronger position than you would have been had they offered $30,000 at the first conference.

**Claude:** ## Refusing to Conciliate

**Do not do this.**

Refusing conciliation puts you in the weakest possible position and here is why.

Conciliation costs you nothing. It is a phone call with a FWC conciliator. You are not obliged to accept any offer made at conciliation — you can attend, hear their number, say no, and walk out. Refusing to attend entirely signals to the Commission that you are the unreasonable party, not Capgemini.

More practically — if you refuse conciliation and the matter proceeds, the Commission member at the hearing will know you refused. That damages your credibility before you have said a word. The entire framing of unfair dismissal is "fair go all round" — an applicant who refuses a free structured negotiation process does not look like someone seeking fairness.

Capgemini refusing is leverage for you because it makes them look unreasonable. You refusing is leverage for them because it makes you look unreasonable. The positions are not symmetrical.

Attend every conciliation. Reject any offer that is too low. Leave the door open. That is strength. Refusing to show up is not.

## The Doctor's Note Question

This needs a direct and honest answer rather than a strategic one.

**Do not obtain a medical certificate for strategic purposes.**

If you are genuinely unwell — if the stress of this situation is continuing to affect your sleep, your mental health, your daily functioning — then you should absolutely see Dr Reed and describe honestly how you are. That is what doctors are for and that is what the certificate reflects.

But if the question is whether you should get a certificate primarily because it strengthens your case rather than because you need medical support — the answer is no, and here is why that matters beyond just the legal risk.

Dr Reed issued your first certificate on 5 March because she assessed you as genuinely unfit. That certificate has significant credibility precisely because it predates the termination call. A second certificate obtained weeks later, timed to coincide with your legal proceedings, looks different. Capgemini's lawyers will note the timing. A Commission member will note the timing. It shifts the certificate from independent clinical evidence — which is powerful — to something that looks calculated, which undermines the first certificate as well.

The psychological harm component of your case rests on a four-month documented deterioration from October 2025, captured in a certificate issued the night before they terminated you. That is a strong and credible medical record. Adding to it artificially risks the integrity of the whole narrative.

**What you should do instead:**

If you are still experiencing symptoms — and given what you have been through it would be entirely normal if you are — book a genuine follow-up with Dr Reed. Describe honestly how you have been feeling since termination. Complete the DASS-21 if you have not already. Let the clinical record reflect your actual state. That is both the right thing to do for your health and the right thing to do for your case.

The honest version of your medical story is already strong. Do not complicate it.

**Claude:** Yes. The F2 form itself is what must be filed by 27 March. Attachments and supporting documents can be provided later.

Here is how it actually works in practice.

---

## The Filing Process

When you submit the F2 online through the Fair Work Commission website, you are filing the application — the claim itself. The form asks for a description of the dismissal, why it was unfair, and your desired outcome. That is what locks in before the deadline.

The supporting documents — witness statements, emails, payslips, the consultation letter, medical certificate, sprint data — are not required at the point of filing. They become relevant at two later stages.

**At conciliation** — you do not formally submit documents. The conciliator facilitates a negotiation. You can refer to your evidence to support your position but there is no formal document exchange.

**If the matter proceeds to a hearing** — the Commission will set a timetable for both parties to file witness statements and documents. That timetable comes after the conciliation has failed or been bypassed. You will typically have several weeks to prepare your evidence bundle.

---

## What This Means Practically

File the F2 by 27 March with the written responses drafted above. Do not delay filing waiting for Justin's statement or any other document.

Then pursue Justin's statement, your mitigation diary, your email evidence bundle, and your medical follow-up in parallel with the conciliation process. By the time you need those documents formally — if the matter goes beyond conciliation — you will have had weeks to compile them properly.

The deadline is for the claim. The evidence comes later.

## Field 2 — Why the Dismissal Was Unfair
*(Reasons — 5,000 characters)*

---

The dismissal does not satisfy the genuine redundancy test under section 389 of the Fair Work Act 2009, and was harsh, unjust and unreasonable under section 387.

**Section 389(1)(a) — The role was still required**

My role as Engagement Manager on the nbn account was not eliminated. The duties I performed — documented in an 8-page handover document I prepared covering all daily, weekly, monthly and quarterly responsibilities — continued to be required after my departure. An offshore individual, Anup Sawant based in Navi Mumbai India, was onboarded from December 2025 to perform those duties. His appointment was announced on 9 December 2025. When I asked my Programme Manager directly in writing whether Anup was stepping into my role, I received no reply. The decision to replace me with an offshore resource was made to reduce cost. That is not a change in operational requirements — it is offshoring for margin. This does not constitute genuine redundancy under the Act.

**Section 389(2) — Reasonable redeployment was available and not genuinely offered**

It would have been reasonable in all the circumstances for me to be redeployed. The employer failed to genuinely assess or offer redeployment opportunities.

(a) SAPN Adelaide role: A specific internal position existed with SA Power Networks in Adelaide. I had directly delivered work for SAPN previously and held relevant industry experience through two additional Capgemini clients in the same sector. I was demonstrably qualified. I formally raised this opportunity in writing on 4 March 2026. I received no written response and no formal assessment of my suitability. On 5 March 2026 — the day before my termination — I was told informally via Teams message that the client wanted the position filled in May. I had purchased annual leave available that would have allowed me to bridge the gap to a May commencement at zero cost to the employer. No written assessment criteria were ever applied to my application for this role. No formal offer was made. The employer's own termination email admits redeployment was unsuccessful. That admission combined with the existence of a suitable role I was qualified for and the absence of any documented assessment process establishes that the redeployment obligation under section 389(2) was not met.

(b) Roles with location restrictions: I identified additional internal roles during the redeployment period. In each instance, requirements were imposed — specifically mandatory attendance at Melbourne or Sydney offices — that were inconsistent with how Capgemini actually operates. Approximately 80% of Capgemini's project delivery teams are based in India and work remotely. I successfully managed national and international clients from South Australia for the entirety of my 4.5-year employment. Imposing physical location restrictions that do not apply to the offshore workforce, and that are inconsistent with my demonstrated working arrangements, represents an unreasonable barrier to redeployment rather than a genuine assessment of suitability.

(c) New Zealand client role: I identified a role with a New Zealand-based client and specifically highlighted that I had previously delivered work for New Zealand clients while based in Adelaide without issue. This was not addressed.

The employer's obligation under section 389(2) is to genuinely assess whether redeployment was reasonable in all the circumstances. The SAPN role was available. I was qualified. A bridging arrangement using purchased leave was entirely feasible. No documented assessment was ever conducted. The redeployment process was not genuine.

**Section 387 — The dismissal was harsh, unjust and unreasonable**

The manner of this dismissal was unreasonable in multiple respects. I received one day's notice of the consultation meeting with no agenda and no documents. My support person's microphone was not functioning and his participation was restricted before the meeting began. The meeting lasted approximately 11 minutes. A consultation letter was issued the same afternoon confirming the decision had already been made before consultation occurred. An 8-day consultation window was provided to an Associate Director with 4.5 years of service. My Programme Manager admitted in writing he deliberately withheld my replacement decision before Christmas — denying me weeks of preparation time. These factors collectively render the dismissal harsh, unjust and unreasonable.

## Description of Dismissal
*(Copy into "Description of dismissal" — 5,000 character limit)*

---

I was employed by Capgemini Australia Pty Ltd (ABN 11 092 284 314) as an Associate Director D2 — Engagement Manager from 31 August 2021 until 6 March 2026, a period of 4 years, 6 months and 6 days. My base salary was $172,542.34 per annum ($3,318.12 per week). No enterprise agreement applied to my employment.

I was assigned to manage two nbn co projects for 14 months. Both projects were failing when I took them over, operating at 25% billing efficiency. Over 34 delivery sprints I improved billing performance to 89% — a 350% improvement — generating $1.17 million in billed revenue. I produced an 8-page written handover document cataloguing my daily, weekly, monthly and quarterly responsibilities, which I emailed to my replacement and Programme Manager and presented across multiple handover meetings.

On 9 December 2025 my Programme Manager announced that Anup Sawant, based in Navi Mumbai India, would be joining as Iteration Manager. I asked in writing whether Anup was stepping into my role. I received no reply. On 20 January 2026 my Programme Manager confirmed in writing I was being moved off the project. He admitted he had deliberately withheld this information before Christmas to avoid ruining my holiday.

From late January 2026 I actively sought internal redeployment. I contacted my line manager five times across February 2026. I identified a specific internal role with SA Power Networks (SAPN) in Adelaide — a client I had directly worked for with direct industry experience — and contacted the hiring manager three or more times without response. I formally raised this redeployment opportunity in writing on 4 March 2026. The following day, 5 March 2026, I was told informally via Teams message that the client wanted the position filled in May. No formal assessment of my suitability was conducted, no written criteria were applied, and no formal offer was made. I had purchased annual leave available that would have allowed me to bridge the gap to a May start at no cost to the employer.

On 25 February 2026 I received a Teams meeting invitation with one day's notice, no agenda, no documents, and no reference to redundancy. My support person attended. His microphone was not working and his participation was restricted before the meeting began. The meeting lasted approximately 11 minutes against a scheduled duration of 20 minutes. A consultation letter was issued the same afternoon. Its file creation metadata will confirm it was prepared before the meeting took place. An 8-day consultation window was provided.

On 6 March 2026 I received a Teams call from the HR Representative and was told I was being made redundant. The written termination email sent at 9:53am stated: "unfortunately we have not been able to redeploy you into a new role." This is Capgemini's own written admission that redeployment failed. My role was not eliminated. It was replaced by an offshore resource to reduce cost.

---

## Reasons for Dismissal — Why the Dismissal Was Unfair
*(Copy into "Reasons for dismissal" — 5,000 character limit)*

---

1. The dismissal does not satisfy the genuine redundancy test under section 389 of the Fair Work Act 2009, and was harsh, unjust and unreasonable under section 387.

2. SECTION 389(1)(a) — THE ROLE WAS STILL REQUIRED. My role as Engagement Manager on the nbn account was not eliminated. The duties I performed — documented in an 8-page handover document covering all daily, weekly, monthly and quarterly responsibilities, emailed to my replacement and Programme Manager — continued to be required after my departure. An offshore individual, Anup Sawant based in Navi Mumbai India, was onboarded from December 2025 to perform those duties. When I asked my Programme Manager directly in writing whether Anup was stepping into my role, I received no reply. The decision to replace me with an offshore resource was a cost reduction decision. That is not a change in operational requirements. It does not constitute genuine redundancy under the Act.

3. SECTION 389(2) — REASONABLE REDEPLOYMENT WAS AVAILABLE AND NOT GENUINELY OFFERED. It would have been reasonable in all circumstances for me to be redeployed. The employer failed to genuinely assess or offer available redeployment opportunities.

4. SAPN Adelaide: A specific internal position existed with SA Power Networks in Adelaide. I had directly delivered work for SAPN and held relevant industry experience through two additional Capgemini clients in the same sector. I was demonstrably qualified and known to the client. I formally raised this opportunity in writing on 4 March 2026. On 5 March 2026 — the day before my termination — I was told informally via Teams message only that the client wanted the position filled in May. No formal assessment of my suitability was conducted. No written criteria were applied. No formal offer was made. I had purchased annual leave available to bridge the gap to a May commencement at zero cost to the employer. Capgemini's own termination email admits redeployment was unsuccessful. That admission combined with the existence of a suitable role I was qualified for and the complete absence of any documented assessment process establishes the obligation under section 389(2) was not met.

5. ROLES WITH LOCATION RESTRICTIONS: I identified additional internal roles during the redeployment period. In each instance requirements were imposed — specifically mandatory attendance at Melbourne or Sydney offices — inconsistent with how Capgemini actually operates. Approximately 80% of Capgemini's project delivery teams are based in India and work remotely. I successfully managed national and international clients from South Australia for the entirety of my 4.5-year employment without issue. Imposing physical location restrictions that do not apply to the offshore workforce and that are inconsistent with my demonstrated working arrangements represents an unreasonable barrier to redeployment rather than a genuine suitability assessment.

6. NEW ZEALAND ROLE: I identified a role with a New Zealand client and specifically highlighted that I had previously delivered work for New Zealand clients while based in Adelaide. This was not addressed by the employer.

7. NO DOCUMENTED REDEPLOYMENT PROCESS: Capgemini has no published internal redundancy or redeployment policy. The absence of a documented process confirms that no structured or genuine redeployment assessment was conducted.

8. SECTION 387 — HARSH, UNJUST AND UNREASONABLE: The manner of this dismissal was unreasonable in multiple respects. I received one day's notice of the consultation meeting with no agenda and no documents. My support person's microphone was not functioning and his participation was restricted before the meeting began. The meeting lasted approximately 11 minutes. A pre-written consultation letter was issued the same afternoon. An 8-day consultation window was provided to an Associate Director with 4.5 years of continuous service. My Programme Manager admitted in writing he deliberately withheld my replacement decision before Christmas — denying me weeks of preparation time. I was never warned of any deficiency in my performance or conduct. My performance record was exemplary — I rescued two failing projects and generated $1.17 million in billed revenue over 14 months.

9. For these reasons the dismissal was not a genuine redundancy and was harsh, unjust and unreasonable.

## Critical Errors — Fix Before Filing

**1. Wrong job title in BOTH witness statements.**
Both statements say "Senior Consultant" in paragraph 1. Your actual role was **Associate Director D2 — Engagement Manager**. This is the role on your employment contract, your payslip, and the role being made redundant. A mismatch between your witness statements and your F2 form will be immediately noticed by Capgemini's lawyers and will undermine credibility. Both documents need paragraph 1 corrected before you file or serve them.

**2. Performance metric inconsistency.**
The F2 says "400% turnaround in project billing." Earlier documents say "350% improvement." These cannot both be correct. You need to confirm the exact figure from your sprint data and use one consistent number across every document. Capgemini will attack any inconsistency.

**3. Employment duration inconsistency.**
The F2 says "five years" in multiple places. The actual period is 4 years, 6 months and 6 days — from 31 August 2021 to 6 March 2026. This needs to be consistent across all documents. The witness statements correctly say 2021 to 2026 but the F2 rounds up to five years. Fix to "over four years" or the precise figure.

---

## Significant Issues — Address Before Filing

**4. Redeployment timeline inconsistency between documents.**
The F2 correctly cites 4 November 2025 as the date you first raised engagement visibility concerns with Vi Tran — giving a four-month lead time. However, Witness Statement 1 paragraph 5 says redeployment efforts started "from approximately 9 February 2026." These two documents are inconsistent on one of your most important arguments. Witness Statement 1 needs to be updated to reference November 2025 in paragraph 5 to align with the F2.

**5. PAL amount discrepancy.**
The F2 desired outcome cites $3,318.12 as the PAL carve-out amount. The claim JSON you provided earlier puts the full PAL claim at $13,317.30 — 20 days of gross salary deductions from January 2025 to February 2026. These are materially different figures. You need to confirm which is correct before filing. If the full amount is $13,317.30, the F2 desired outcome needs to be updated accordingly.

**6. F2 document has duplicate content.**
The F2 document contains two versions of the same content — a full detailed version followed by what appears to be a shorter revised version. If you are copying from this document into the online form, only one version should be used. The first detailed version is stronger and more comprehensive.

**7. Reinstatement as primary remedy — tactical conflict with Jason's advice.**
The F2 seeks reinstatement as the primary remedy. Jason explicitly advised not to seek reinstatement but to use it as a bargaining chip — present it as something you are aware of and reserve, not as what you actually want. Seeking reinstatement formally can complicate your position if the Commission takes it at face value. The recommended approach is to frame it as: "I am aware reinstatement is the primary remedy and reserve it, but seek compensation." This is already the framing in the responses drafted earlier in this conversation. The F2 as written goes further than that — you need to decide which approach you want before filing.

---

## New Facts Not Yet in the JSON

**8. AGIG smoking gun — strongest new argument.**
The F2 contains a powerful piece of evidence not previously captured in the JSON: Capgemini told you the AGIG (Australian Gas Infrastructure Group) role was unsuitable because it was Melbourne-based. But AGIG is headquartered at **400 King William Street, Adelaide** — a site you had worked at for 18 months for a different client and were fully site-ready for. This directly contradicts the location excuse and exposes it as a pretext. This needs to go into the JSON and should be in your F2 field responses.

**9. PIP from 2024 — Western Power.**
The F2 voluntarily discloses that you were placed on a Performance Improvement Plan in mid-2024 following Western Power, which you passed. This is a double-edged disclosure. On one hand it shows you overcame it and went on to exceptional performance. On the other hand it gives Capgemini a performance history point they may not have intended to use. You should think carefully about whether to include this. If Capgemini raises it themselves in their response, you have already addressed it. If they do not raise it, you have handed them a line of argument. This needs a decision.

**10. Contracts clauses 8.1 and 9.1.**
The F2 references clauses 8.1 and 9.1 of your employment contract in support of remote work and location arguments. These are not in the JSON. What do these clauses actually say? If they expressly permit remote work or do not restrict you to a specific location, they are valuable. If they are more ambiguous, citing them specifically in the F2 could invite scrutiny. Confirm the exact wording before the document is filed.

**11. 10:22pm email to Anle on 25 February.**
The F2 references an email you sent to Anle Pretorius at 10:22 PM on 25 February 2026 requesting the meeting be recorded and offering to reschedule to Friday. Both requests were ignored. This is strong procedural evidence and is not in the JSON. It needs to be added.

**12. Four separate support person contacts.**
The F2 mentions you contacted four separate individuals before finding Justin as your support person — all unavailable at such short notice. This is additional evidence of the unreasonably short notice for the consultation meeting and should be preserved.

## Could It Be More

Possibly yes — on two grounds.

**Statutory interest.** The F2 desired outcome references "plus statutory interest" on both amounts. Interest on unpaid entitlements from the date they fell due adds to the total. The PAL deductions were taken progressively from January 2025 — interest on the earliest deductions has been accruing for over a year. This is a legitimate addition to the claim but needs to be calculated precisely.

**The leave clawback.** The termination pay included a deduction of approximately $5,010.46 described as a negative annual leave balance — your PAL scheme permitted you to take leave in advance. Whether this clawback was lawful given that the PAL scheme itself was never properly administered and the employer is simultaneously refusing to refund the contributions is a live question. If the clawback was unlawful, the total claim increases by $5,010.46. This needs a lawyer to assess.

**You:** is this good for me or them? Contract Clauses 8.1 and 9.1 — Now Confirmed
These are from the Empired contract which transferred to Capgemini via the July 2022 letter ("All other terms and conditions remain unchanged").
Clause 8.1: Primary place of work is the Adelaide office. May be required to work at other locations including client premises. This directly contradicts any claim that Melbourne office attendance was a contractual requirement.
